Translated text

¶284 Munnu: 'By God, the house is empty. You suspect us? At your command we would leap into fire.'Munnu: 'By God, the house is empty. You suspect us? At your command we would leap into fire.'

¶285 She alighted and entered. Even caught, the bird fluttered once, but birdlime on its feet prevented flight, and the hunter put it in his bag. Would the unfortunate one ever fly or chirp on branches again? Shyam returned in the morning, calmer, fearing she might be absent. Both door leaves open made his heart go numb—an evil sign so early. He listened: silence. Courtyard, upstairs, all deserted; the house seemed to bite. Looking carefully, he found money gone, jewel box empty. No doubt now; one doesn't take savings for a Ganges bath. She had left, and he knew where. Perhaps hurrying could bring her back, but what would people say? Sitting on a cot, he coolly analyzed. Surely Raza and his follower Munnu misled her. What else was his duty? He moved house, repeatedly reasoned. What more? Was beating improper? Suppose it was for a moment; should she leave? Would a woman not already poisoned leave merely from blows? Surely her heart was tainted.She alighted and entered. Shyam returned in the morning, calmer, fearing she might be absent. Both door leaves open made his heart go numb—an evil sign so early. He listened: silence. Courtyard, upstairs, all deserted; the house seemed to bite. Looking carefully, he found money gone, jewel box empty. No doubt now; one doesn't take savings for a Ganges bath. She had left, and he knew where. Perhaps hurrying could bring her back, but what would people say? Sitting on a cot, he coolly analyzed. Surely Raza and his follower Munnu misled her. What else was his duty? He moved house, repeatedly reasoned. What more? Was beating improper? Suppose it was for a moment; should she leave? Would a woman not already poisoned leave merely from blows? Surely her heart was tainted.

¶286 Then he thought the maid would come, ask, and spread the news instantly. God, what to do? He felt no remorse or pity. Could he find her, he would not hesitate to kill. Leaving, even from passion alone, was unforgivable. Anger often becomes detachment. He hated the world. When one's wife betrays, what hope of anyone? The woman for whom one lives and dies, sacrifices life for happiness—if not one's own, who is? What had he not done to please her? Fought family, severed brothers' ties until they would not see him, fulfilled every wish. A headache terrified him; he nursed her all night. She betrayed him at a ruffian's prompting, blackened his face. Yet blaming ruffians merely consoled: who can mislead an untainted heart? If she deceived, love and trust did not exist, merely sentimental imagination. Such a world offered misery and frustrated hopes. Wicked woman! Free now, do as you will; nobody holds your hand. This crooked treatment of him she endlessly called beloved! He could drag her to court for punishment, but why? God would repay it.Then he thought the maid would come, ask, and spread the news instantly. God, what to do? He felt no remorse or pity. Could he find her, he would not hesitate to kill. Leaving, even from passion alone, was unforgivable. Anger often becomes detachment. He hated the world. When one's wife betrays, what hope of anyone? The woman for whom one lives and dies, sacrifices life for happiness—if not one's own, who is? What had he not done to please her? Fought family, severed brothers' ties until they would not see him, fulfilled every wish. A headache terrified him; he nursed her all night. She betrayed him at a ruffian's prompting, blackened his face. Yet blaming ruffians merely consoled: who can mislead an untainted heart? If she deceived, love and trust did not exist, merely sentimental imagination. Such a world offered misery and frustrated hopes. Wicked woman! Free now, do as you will; nobody holds your hand. This crooked treatment of him she endlessly called beloved! He could drag her to court for punishment, but why? God would repay it.

¶287 Silently he descended, said nothing to anyone, left the doors open, and walked toward the Ganges.Silently he descended, said nothing to anyone, left the doors open, and walked toward the Ganges.

Coverage and translation notes

Accusations and narrative perspective
Shyam’s suspicions and later self-justifications are translated as his thoughts. They are not established proof of Devi’s infidelity. The ending explicitly portrays her vulnerability to a trap.
Names and setting
Raja Bhaiya is Sharda’s affectionate name for Raza, not a separate man. Devi and Devirani refer to the same woman. Caste-associated terms and prejudiced statements remain attributed to their speakers.
Printed fragments
Several sentences are divided across source paragraphs. English preserves those units with connecting dashes. The concluding reference to the Ganges is retained although an earlier city setting might make another river seem expected.
Ending
The source ends with Shyam leaving the doors open and walking toward the river; no later fate for either spouse is supplied.
